Stone identifies two serious routes: exclusion of the local conduct might materially impair or permit evasion of a valid interstate regulatory regime, or the conduct might create a substantial interstate externality that individual States cannot reasonably internalize — the record must actually support whichever mechanism the Government invokes.

**What this establishes.** Operationalizes the questions *Lopez* left open; one of the most important Stone cases in the entire project.

#### Wisconsin Department of Corrections v. Schacht, 524 U.S. 381 (1998)

Source: equality build, under "OT1997: decisions principally issued in 1998".

**Historical question.** Did the presence of a potentially immune state claim prevent removal of the entire action to federal court?

**Stone's disposition.** Reverse that categorical jurisdictional rule.

**Reasoning.** Assess the actual jurisdiction and immunity of each claim. State immunity is not a device for erasing federal jurisdiction over different, otherwise cognizable claims. Preserve the defense where it properly applies. [Opinion](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/524/381/).

### OT1998

#### Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank, 527 U.S. 627 (1999)

Source: commerce build, under "OT1998".

**Status.** Historical Supreme Court merits case.

**Historical questions.** Did Congress validly abrogate state immunity from the patent-infringement damages action through § 5 of the Fourteenth Amendment?

**Stone's disposition.** Affirm dismissal of the direct damages action against the nonconsenting state entity.

**Simulation result.** Affirmed, 5-4 (Stevens, joined by Stone, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer). Section 5 (Part II) and Article I (Part III) each independently sustain the expressly authorized patent action.

**Reasoning.** The Patent Clause validly creates substantive federal patent obligations, but *Seminole Tribe* means Article I does not itself authorize a private damages suit against the State. Section 5 is a separate source and must be justified on its own terms. Congress did not establish a sufficiently concrete pattern of States depriving patent owners of property without constitutionally adequate process, and the abrogation rule reaches infringement categorically rather than tracking intentional or otherwise unconstitutional deprivations and the availability of process. The defect is remedial fit, not the validity of federal patent law. Waiver, federal enforcement, and properly available prospective officer relief remain distinct routes.

**What this establishes.** A federal right can bind a State while a particular private damages remedy fails for want of a valid abrogation source. Stone will not manufacture a Fourteenth Amendment violation merely to preserve a remedy Congress attempted to rest on another power.

#### Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank, 527 U.S. 627 (1999)

Source: equality build, under "OT1998: decisions principally issued in 1999".

**Historical question.** Did Congress validly remove state immunity for the patent-infringement damages action?

**Stone's disposition.** Reverse the Federal Circuit and dismiss the direct damages action against the nonconsenting state entity on sovereign-immunity grounds.

**Reasoning.** *Seminole Tribe* removes the Article I shortcut. The Patent Clause validly imposes federal patent obligations on States, but a private damages action against the State requires waiver or a separate constitutional source for abrogation. Section 5 does not supply that source here. Congress did not identify a sufficiently developed pattern of States intentionally or otherwise unconstitutionally depriving patent owners of property without adequate process, while the remedy applies to infringement categorically without tracking unconstitutional deprivation, culpability, or the availability of process. Patent infringement and a Fourteenth Amendment deprivation therefore remain distinct legal concepts. The patent duty survives; waiver, federal enforcement, and properly available prospective officer relief remain separate mechanisms. [Opinion and dissents](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/527/627/).

#### College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board, 527 U.S. 666 (1999)

Source: commerce build, under "OT1998".

**Status.** Historical Supreme Court merits case.

**Historical questions.** Did Congress validly abrogate immunity for the Lanham Act false-advertising claim, and did Florida constructively waive immunity by entering a federally regulated commercial market?

**Stone's disposition.** Affirm the immunity dismissal.

**Simulation result.** Reversed and remanded, 5-4, without a common rationale. Stone's opinion of the Court holds that §5 does not support the own-product false-advertising theory (Part II, eight votes) and that continued commerce is not consent (Part III, five votes); his Part IV, joined by no one, would sustain the suit under Article I.

**Reasoning.** Congress clearly sought to authorize the action, but the Commerce Clause cannot supply the abrogation after *Seminole Tribe*, and this false-advertising claim supplies no independent § 5 basis for retrospective damages against the State. Market participation is not consent to suit. Waiver must be an actual, voluntary, and sufficiently clear choice by the State, not a legal fiction attached to conducting business in an area Congress regulates. Congress remains free to regulate the conduct substantively and to use constitutionally available enforcement mechanisms.

**What this establishes.** Compulsory abrogation and voluntary waiver are separate doctrines. Neither can be created by relabeling the other.

#### College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid, 527 U.S. 666 (1999)

Source: equality build, under "OT1998: decisions principally issued in 1999".

**Historical questions.** Did Congress validly abrogate immunity for the Lanham Act false-advertising claim? Had the State waived immunity by participating in the relevant market?

**Stone's disposition.** Affirm the immunity dismissal on both theories.

**Reasoning.** Congress clearly attempted to authorize the action, but after *Seminole Tribe* the Commerce Clause does not provide power to make a nonconsenting State a private damages defendant. The false-advertising claim also supplies no independent § 5 basis for retrospective state liability. Waiver is different from abrogation and must be an actual, voluntary, sufficiently clear choice by the State. Participation in a federally regulated market cannot be converted into fictional consent to suit merely because Congress could regulate the activity. Substantive federal false-advertising rules may still govern the State's conduct through constitutionally available enforcement channels. [Opinion and dissents](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/527/666/).

#### Alden v. Maine, 527 U.S. 706 (1999)

Source: commerce build, under "OT1998".

**Status.** Historical Supreme Court merits case.

**Historical questions.** Could Congress authorize private FLSA damages actions against a nonconsenting State in the State's own courts?

**Stone's disposition.** Affirm Maine's immunity dismissal.

**Simulation result.** Reversed and remanded, 5-4 (Souter, joined by Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer). The commerce power displaces Maine's immunity from the expressly authorized FLSA suit in its own courts; Hilton is extended.

**Reasoning.** The immunity principle recognized in *Seminole Tribe* is not merely a venue rule for federal courts. Congress may impose valid Article I employment duties on a State, but it may not use Article I alone to make the nonconsenting State a private damages defendant in its own courts. Public federal enforcement, valid Reconstruction-Amendment abrogation, actual waiver, and properly available prospective officer relief remain separate mechanisms. To the extent Stone's earlier *Hilton* decision treated FELA coverage and statutory stare decisis as sufficient to compel a nonconsenting State to answer a private Article I damages action in state court, *Alden* openly rejects that remedial proposition while leaving the substantive federal railroad duty intact. Constitutional power cannot be supplied by statutory stare decisis.

**What this establishes.** The administrable immunity sequence is source of duty → private action → defendant and forum → immunity, waiver, or valid abrogation → authorized relief. The failure of one enforcement route does not erase the underlying federal obligation, but the existence of that obligation does not create a constitutionally unavailable damages action.

#### Alden v. Maine, 527 U.S. 706 (1999)

Source: equality build, under "OT1998: decisions principally issued in 1999".

**Historical question.** Could Congress authorize the employees' FLSA action against a nonconsenting State in its own courts?

**Stone's disposition.** Affirm Maine's immunity dismissal of the private damages action.

**Reasoning.** Congress may validly regulate a State as an employer under Article I without thereby acquiring authority to subject the nonconsenting State to a private damages suit. The immunity principle is constitutional structure rather than a federal-forum venue rule, so moving the same Article I damages action into state court does not cure the defect identified in *Seminole Tribe*. Public federal enforcement, actual waiver, valid Reconstruction-Amendment abrogation, and properly available prospective officer relief remain separate mechanisms. The substantive FLSA obligation is not erased merely because this private enforcement route is unavailable.

**Doctrinal change from the earlier build.** This decision must also confront *Hilton* candidly. To the extent Stone's earlier *Hilton* holding treated FELA coverage and statutory stare decisis as sufficient to compel a nonconsenting State to answer a private Article I damages action in its own courts, that remedial proposition is overruled. FELA's substantive coverage of state-owned railroads can remain, but statutory precedent cannot create constitutional power to impose a private damages forum that Article I does not supply. The governing sequence is source of duty → private action → defendant and forum → immunity, waiver, or valid abrogation → relief.

### OT1999

Cross-reference: the commerce build's entry "Fraternal Order of Police v. United States, 173 F.3d 898" is placed under Second Amendment, OT1999, beside the Second Amendment build's entry for that case.

Cross-reference: the commerce build's entry "Gillespie v. City of Indianapolis, 185 F.3d 693" is placed under Second Amendment, OT1999, beside the Second Amendment build's entry for that case.

#### Kimel v. Florida Board of Regents, 528 U.S. 62 (2000)

Source: commerce build, under "OT1999".

**Status.** Historical Supreme Court merits case.

**Historical questions.** Did the ADEA clearly authorize private damages suits against States, and did § 5 of the Fourteenth Amendment give Congress power to abrogate immunity for those suits?

**Stone's disposition.** Affirm the immunity dismissal of the private retrospective damages claims.

**Simulation result.** Reversed and remanded, 5-4 (Ginsburg, joined by Stone, Stevens, Souter and Breyer). Article I sustains the ADEA suit against the State; §5 is reserved.

**Reasoning.** Congress clearly included state employers, so the case turns on constitutional power rather than statutory ambiguity. Age classifications ordinarily violate equal protection only when irrational, while the ADEA broadly forbids many age distinctions that a State could constitutionally make. The state-employment record does not establish a pattern of unconstitutional age discrimination sufficient to justify that breadth as § 5 prevention or remedy. The ADEA's substantive employment rule may still rest on the Commerce Clause; federal enforcement and other constitutionally available remedies remain. What fails is this private damages abrogation, not the federal employment standard itself.

**What this establishes.** *Boerne* asks whether the § 5 remedy is fitted to the constitutional wrong Congress may prevent or remedy. A valid Article I duty neither supplies nor destroys the separate immunity analysis.

#### Kimel v. Florida Board of Regents, 528 U.S. 62 (2000)

Source: equality build, under "OT1999: decisions principally issued in 2000".

**Historical questions.** Did the ADEA clearly authorize private suits against States, and did Congress possess constitutional authority for that abrogation?

**Stone's disposition.** Affirm the immunity dismissal of the private retrospective damages claims.

**Reasoning.** The ADEA clearly includes state employers, so Stone answers the power question rather than hiding behind statutory ambiguity. *Seminole Tribe* and *Alden* foreclose Article I as the abrogation source. Section 5 also does not sustain this remedy. Age classifications generally violate equal protection only when irrational, while the ADEA broadly prohibits many age distinctions that the Constitution permits. The state-employment record does not establish a recurring pattern of unconstitutional age discrimination strong enough to justify that breadth as a preventive § 5 damages remedy. This does not invalidate the ADEA's substantive regulation of state employment under the Commerce Clause or foreclose federal enforcement and other constitutionally available remedies. [Opinion and Stevens dissent](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/528/62/).

#### Reno v. Condon, 528 U.S. 141 (2000)

Source: commerce build, under "OT1999".

**Status.** Historical Supreme Court merits case, major anti-commandeering clarification.

**Historical questions.** Does a federal law restricting states' disclosure of driver's-license information commandeer state governments?

**Stone's disposition.** Uphold federal regulation.

**Reasoning.** South Carolina's motor-vehicle department is itself supplying and disseminating information in an interstate market — Congress is regulating the State's own conduct as an information holder/supplier. That differs from *Printz*: no State officer is being ordered to administer a federal regulatory program against private third parties. Administrative work necessary to comply with a direct federal rule is not commandeering merely because the regulated actor is a State.

**What this establishes.** Prevents *Printz* from mutating into immunity of States from ordinary federal regulation.

#### Reno v. Condon, 528 U.S. 141 (2000)

Source: equality build, under "OT1999: decisions principally issued in 2000".

**Historical question.** Did the Driver's Privacy Protection Act's restrictions on disclosure of personal motor-vehicle data violate federalism limits?

**Stone's disposition.** Reverse invalidation and uphold the statute as applied to the state databases.

**Reasoning.** Congress regulated the state's activity involving information in interstate commerce rather than conscripting the state to regulate its citizens. This implements the line preserved in *Printz* and shows that administrable federalism does not require categorical immunity for state institutions. [Opinion](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/528/141/).

#### United States v. Locke, 529 U.S. 89 (2000)

Source: commerce build, under "OT1999".

**Status.** Historical Supreme Court merits case.

**Historical questions.** Does federal maritime law occupy the field of oil-tanker safety regulation, preempting state rules?

**Stone's position.** Identify the particular subject of state tanker regulation and determine whether federal maritime law actually occupies it. National uniformity matters where Congress created a national rule — it is not an incantation. Distinctively local waterway conditions can remain subject to local authority where federal law has not occupied the subject and no actual conflict exists.

**What this establishes.** Field preemption must be tied to statutory architecture, not administrative preference.

#### United States v. Morrison, 529 U.S. 598 (2000)

Source: commerce build, under "OT1999".

**Status.** Historical Supreme Court merits case, major Commerce limit.

**Historical questions.** Did the Violence Against Women Act's federal civil remedy for gender-motivated violence exceed the commerce power?

**Stone's disposition.** Invalidate the federal civil remedy under the asserted Commerce theory.

**Reasoning.** Gender-motivated violence is enormously serious; that seriousness does not supply the constitutional source. The Government's theory again depends on generalized consequences — violence affects victims, employment, travel, medical spending, productivity, insurance, and the economy — which is *Lopez*'s forbidden consequence chain. Congress may regulate interstate travel, transactions, market obstruction, interstate criminal enterprises, and other genuinely interstate mechanisms; it cannot regulate every local violent act because violence in aggregate has economic consequences.

**What this establishes.** Proves *Lopez* is a real limit even in a case involving major national harm.

#### United States v. Morrison, 529 U.S. 598 (2000)

Source: equality build, under "OT1999: decisions principally issued in 2000".

**Historical question.** Could Congress authorize the VAWA civil damages action against private perpetrators through the Commerce Clause or Fourteenth Amendment §5?

**Stone's disposition.** Affirm invalidation of that particular civil remedy under those asserted powers.

**Reasoning.** The evidentiary record concerning violence and failures in state justice systems was serious. Nevertheless, the enacted cause of action directly targeted private perpetrators without making the remedy depend on a constitutionally relevant state violation or a sufficient interstate-commercial connection. A record documenting a grave problem does not itself authorize every chosen federal remedy. Stone distinguishes valid protection against state electoral discrimination and Congress's regulation of states as employers. He does not hold that Congress is powerless to address gender violence through properly grounded criminal, spending, or state-accountability measures. [Opinion and dissents](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/99-5.ZO.html).

#### Jones v. United States, 529 U.S. 848 (2000)

Source: commerce build, under "OT1999".

**Status.** Historical Supreme Court merits case.

**Historical questions.** Is an owner-occupied family home "used" in interstate commerce within the federal arson statute, § 844(i)?

**Stone's disposition.** Reverse through statutory construction.

**Reasoning.** A home is not actively "used" in interstate commerce merely because it has insurance, utilities, a mortgage, supplies purchased across state lines, and other ordinary incidents of modern home ownership. No constitutional holding is necessary; *Lopez* serves as avoidance context, confining § 844(i) to active commercial use.

**What this establishes.** A canonical example of restraint — when Congress's statute can fairly be read narrowly, do that before announcing another constitutional boundary. Matters heavily in *SWANCC* and *Bond II*.

#### Geier v. American Honda, 529 U.S. 861 (2000)

Source: commerce build, under "OT1999".

**Status.** Historical Supreme Court merits case.

**Historical questions.** Does a federal safety standard giving manufacturers a choice among restraint systems preempt a state tort theory that would have required one particular option?

**Stone's disposition.** Find conflict with the particular tort theory.

**Reasoning.** *Myrick* remains true — absence of federal regulation does not itself preempt. But here federal regulators affirmatively selected a mixed restraint strategy as part of the means of achieving the federal objective; a state tort rule effectively requiring every manufacturer to use the option federal regulators deliberately chose not to require would defeat that operative federal method.

**What this establishes.** Stone's restrictive approach to preemption is not anti-preemption — actual federal policy can preempt; the key word is actual.

#### Crosby v. National Foreign Trade Council, 530 U.S. 363 (2000)

Source: commerce build, under "OT1999".

**Status.** Historical Supreme Court merits case.

**Historical questions.** Did a state sanctions law targeting companies doing business with Burma conflict with a federal sanctions statute covering the same subject?

**Stone's disposition.** Invalidate the conflicting Massachusetts structure.

**Reasoning.** Congress enacted a federal sanctions regime assigning the Executive particular flexibility, scope, exceptions, and diplomatic tools. Massachusetts cannot expand the sanctions, remove federally assigned flexibility, and thereby alter the method Congress chose.

**What this establishes.** Concrete statutory conflict, not a free-standing judicial "foreign affairs preemption" doctrine.

#### Bradley v. Arkansas Department of Education

Source: commerce build, under "OT1999".

**Status.** Lower-court sim case.

**Historical questions.** Did Arkansas's acceptance of federal special-education funding constitute genuine consent to private federal enforcement of IDEA?

**Stone's disposition.** Uphold Arkansas's program-specific consent on this record.

**Reasoning.** The funding condition is prospective, program-related, clearly stated, and unmistakably tells participating States that private federal enforcement accompanies acceptance — genuine funding consent rather than *College Savings*-style constructive waiver from merely engaging in regulated activity.

**What this establishes.** The lawful-consent side of Spending Clause federalism, before confronting coercion; later qualified by *Kansas* — formal ability to refuse is not always practical voluntariness.

#### Bradley v. Arkansas Department of Education, 189 F.3d 745 (8th Cir. 1999)

Source: equality build, under "OT1999: decisions principally issued in 2000".

**Simulation vehicle: OT1999, June 29, 2000.**

**Historical questions.** Did state immunity bar the IDEA claims and the consolidated §504 Rehabilitation Act claims? Did Congress provide valid abrogation or sufficiently clear funding conditions and consent to suit?

**Stone's disposition.** Permit the IDEA claims and the related §504 claims against the covered recipient to proceed on the clear funding-condition/waiver basis; preserve appropriate prospective officer relief. Reject the categorical proposition that a statute exceeding the constitutional minimum necessarily exceeds Congress's power.

**Reasoning.** The actual reported opinion addressed both IDEA and Rehabilitation Act claims, not merely the abstract validity of a spending condition. Read the IDEA's immunity and judicial-review provisions together; identify the relevant recipient and the terms attached to accepting funds. For §504, the statutory notice provision and the scope of the funded agency matter. Voluntary acceptance of a clear program condition is distinct from the market-participation “constructive waiver” rejected as unnecessary in *College Savings*. The later en banc *Jim C.* litigation must not be silently substituted for this earlier decision. [Actual consolidated opinion](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/189/745/526628/).

### OT2000

#### Solid Waste Agency of Northern Cook County v. Army Corps of Engineers (SWANCC), 531 U.S. 159 (2001)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2000".

**Status.** Historical Supreme Court merits case.

**Historical questions.** Does the Clean Water Act's jurisdiction reach isolated, nonnavigable, wholly intrastate ponds used by migratory birds?

**Stone's disposition.** Reject federal jurisdiction through statutory construction.

**Reasoning.** The Act should not be read to reach such waters solely because migratory birds use them; Stone does not need to declare the outer Commerce power, since the statute's water-based jurisdictional language provides the narrower ground.

**What this establishes.** *Jones* applied to federal environmental regulation — *Jones → SWANCC → Bond II* becomes an important Stone avoidance sequence.

#### Board of Trustees of the University of Alabama v. Garrett, 531 U.S. 356 (2001)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2000".

**Status.** Historical Supreme Court merits case.

**Historical questions.** Did Congress validly abrogate state sovereign immunity for ADA Title I employment-discrimination damages under § 5 of the Fourteenth Amendment?

**Stone's disposition.** Reject the immunity defense to the Title I damages actions and permit the claims to proceed.

**Reasoning.** The answer is not supplied by the Commerce Clause after *Seminole Tribe*; Stone reaches § 5 directly. Congress assembled a substantial record of exclusion and stereotyped treatment of people with disabilities by public institutions, including employment, and chose a bounded preventive rule rather than an unlimited entitlement. Title I protects qualified individuals, permits job-related qualification standards, requires only reasonable accommodation, and preserves an undue-hardship defense. Those limits make the remedy sufficiently fitted to the recurring constitutional danger of irrational exclusion and stereotype-based state action. This does not make disability a suspect classification, convert every failure to accommodate into an independent constitutional violation, or validate every application of the ADA without regard to statutory defenses.

**What this establishes.** *Kimel* and *Garrett* turn on proof and fit, not on a categorical rule that prophylactic § 5 legislation is either always valid or always invalid. Congress receives room to prevent recurring unconstitutional state discrimination when the evidentiary record and statutory limits identify an administrable connection to the constitutional wrong.

#### Board of Trustees of the University of Alabama v. Garrett, 531 U.S. 356 (2001)

Source: equality build, under "OT2000: decisions principally issued in 2001".

**Historical questions.** Could state employees recover money damages under ADA Title I, given sovereign immunity and Congress's asserted enforcement authority? What should the Court do with the portion of the grant concerning Title II employment claims when the antecedent statutory-coverage issue was not adequately briefed?

**Stone's disposition.** Affirm the appellate rejection of the Title I immunity barrier and allow those damages claims to proceed because the Title I abrogation is valid § 5 enforcement. Dismiss the inadequately presented Title II portion as improvidently granted.

**Reasoning.** Stone reaches the § 5 question directly; Article I cannot do the remedial work after *Seminole Tribe*. Congress compiled a substantial record of exclusion, stereotype, and unequal treatment of people with disabilities by public institutions, including employment, and Title I responds through bounded rules rather than an unlimited preference. The claimant must be qualified; employers may maintain job-related standards; accommodation must be reasonable; and undue hardship remains a defense. Those limitations make the remedy sufficiently fitted to the recurring constitutional danger of irrational and stereotype-based exclusion by state employers.

The rule is broader than the constitutional minimum, as preventive legislation often is, but it is not detached from that minimum. Stone does not make disability a suspect classification or hold that every failure to accommodate is independently unconstitutional. The distinction from *Kimel* is evidentiary and remedial: Congress identified a more substantial pattern of constitutionally suspect exclusion and used qualification and hardship limits that focus the preventive rule on the mechanism producing that exclusion. [Historical opinion](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/99-1240.ZO.html); [Breyer dissent](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/99-1240.ZD.html).

#### Buckman Co. v. Plaintiffs' Legal Committee, 531 U.S. 341 (2001)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2000".

**Status.** Historical Supreme Court merits case.

**Historical questions.** Does federal law preempt a state tort claim alleging fraud on the FDA in the medical-device approval process?

**Stone's disposition.** Fraud-on-the-FDA theory preempted; independently grounded traditional state duties preserved.

**Reasoning.** If the claimed duty exists only because of a party's relationship with a federal agency, allowing a private state-law action to police that federal relationship can interfere with Congress's chosen federal enforcement structure. But traditional state tort duties do not disappear merely because the same conduct also violated federal law.

**What this establishes.** Matures the duty-specific preemption method.

#### Kansas v. United States

Source: commerce build, under "OT2000".

**Status.** Lower-court sim case, foundational Spending case.

**Historical questions.** Did a federal funding condition become unconstitutionally coercive?

**Stone's disposition.** Reverse and remand; reject Kansas's notice and relatedness objections, but the coercion challenge survives.

**Reasoning.** The conditions are prospective and programmatically related, but a funding condition becomes coercive when the structure and magnitude of the threatened loss materially eliminate the State's reasonably practicable ability to decline, turning a formal offer into the practical equivalent of a command. No fixed percentage controls; relevant circumstances include the amount and centrality of funding, dependence on an established stream, whether the Government is offering genuinely new money or threatening entrenched funds to obtain new obligations, transition possibilities, alternatives, scope and newness of the condition, and genuine waiver/safety valves. Kansas bears the ultimate burden.

**What this establishes.** Answers the question *Printz* deliberately left open. Formal consent is not enough — consent has to remain practically real.

#### Kansas v. United States, 214 F.3d 1196 (10th Cir. 2000)

Source: equality build, under "OT2000: decisions principally issued in 2001".

**Simulation vehicle: OT2000, April 16, 2001.**

**Historical question.** Did the federal child-support enforcement requirements and their relationship to federal welfare funding exceed the Spending Clause, including through coercion?

**Stone's disposition.** Affirm rejection of the state's facial challenge on the historical record.

**Reasoning.** Identify the actual statutory conditions, notice, programmatic relationship, and financial choice. A substantial incentive is not automatically coercion. Do not declare coercion conceptually impossible or establish a universal percentage threshold. This is a funding condition, distinct from the direct executive command invalidated in *Printz*. [Actual appellate opinion](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/214/1196/533464/).

#### United States v. Oakland Cannabis Buyers' Cooperative, 532 U.S. 483 (2001)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2000".

**Status.** Historical Supreme Court merits case, statutory precursor.

**Historical questions.** Does the Controlled Substances Act contain an implied medical-necessity defense?

**Stone's position.** Courts cannot create a general medical-necessity exception Congress did not enact — this is primarily statutory, not the eventual *Raich* Commerce case, and should not prejudge whether Congress constitutionally reaches every purely local marijuana activity.

**What this establishes.** Keeps statutory exceptions separate from constitutional power.

#### Gibbs v. Babbitt

Source: commerce build, under "OT2000".

**Status.** Lower-court sim case, major Commerce application.

**Historical questions.** Does application of the Endangered Species Act's take prohibition to a landowner killing a wolf on private property survive under the *Home Builders* framework?

**Stone's disposition.** Vacate and remand.

**Reasoning.** The economic motivation of the landowner does not convert taking a wolf into economic activity; tourism, research, possible future scientific value, and the Act's comprehensive character do not automatically establish the interstate mechanism. Apply *Home Builders*: can exclusion of this conduct materially impair or permit evasion of a valid interstate regime, or does it create a substantial interstate externality that individual States cannot reasonably internalize? The lower courts did not make the required findings, so remand rather than invent the facts.

**What this establishes.** Proves the *Home Builders* evidentiary mechanism is real and courts cannot manufacture it.

#### United States v. Angle (grant)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2000".

**Status.** Lower-court sim case; certiorari granted June 25, 2001, merits decided the following Term.

**Historical questions.** Can intrastate possession of actual child pornography be federalized because production materials previously traveled through interstate commerce, or as an anti-evasion component of the interstate trafficking regime?

**Stone's disposition.** Do not resolve until OT2001.

### OT2001

#### Michigan v. Environmental Protection Agency

Source: commerce build, under "OT2001".

**Status.** Lower-court sim case.

**Historical questions.** Did EPA's regional ozone-transport rule properly establish upwind States' contribution to downwind nonattainment, and did the cooperative-federalism implementation structure commandeer the States?

**Stone's disposition.** Affirm in part, vacate in part, remand.

**Reasoning.** EPA must first establish a measurable causal contribution from an upwind State to downwind nonattainment; cost-effectiveness can then help determine how much contribution reasonably must be eliminated, but cost cannot create the interstate linkage the statute requires. On federalism, EPA can establish a valid statewide emissions outcome while leaving the State free to choose sources, controls, trading arrangements, schedules, and other lawful implementation methods; if the State declines, the statute provides a federal implementation fallback, which is not commandeering.

**What this establishes.** What legitimate cooperative federalism looks like: federal outcome, genuine State implementation choice, federal fallback.

#### Lapides v. Board of Regents, 535 U.S. 613 (2002)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2001".

**Status.** Historical Supreme Court merits case.

**Historical questions.** Does a State's voluntary removal of a case to federal court waive its sovereign-immunity objection to that forum?

**Stone's disposition.** Voluntary removal waives only the federal-forum objection for claims already amenable to adjudication.

**Reasoning.** A State that affirmatively invokes federal jurisdiction cannot then object to that same federal forum for the claims it voluntarily brought there — but the waiver does not create a cause of action, damages, substantive liability, federal statutory personhood, or surrender of unrelated defenses.

**What this establishes.** Keeps "waiver" from becoming an amorphous fairness doctrine; ask exactly what the State voluntarily did and what objection that act relinquished.

#### Lapides v. Board of Regents, 535 U.S. 613 (2002)

Source: equality build, under "OT2001: decisions from late 2001 through 2002".

**Historical question.** Did a state's voluntary removal waive its Eleventh Amendment objection to state-law claims for which it had already consented to suit in its own courts?

**Stone's disposition.** Reverse the contrary immunity ruling at that limited scope.

**Reasoning.** A state may not voluntarily invoke the federal forum and then use the forum choice to defeat the already permitted claims. Do not turn this into a decision that removal waives every possible immunity for every unconsented federal claim. Stone's broader congressional-abrogation rule remains a separate route. [Opinion](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/535/613/).

#### Verizon Maryland v. Public Service Commission, 535 U.S. 635 (2002)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2001".

**Status.** Historical Supreme Court merits case.

**Historical questions.** May a party obtain prospective relief against state commissioners for enforcing an order contrary to federal telecommunications law?

**Stone's disposition.** Allow prospective federal relief against responsible commissioners.

**Reasoning.** A properly pleaded claim that state commissioners are presently enforcing an order contrary to controlling federal law can proceed through prospective officer relief; a detailed federal statute displaces that ordinary equitable route only where Congress actually made another channel exclusive for the same duty and relief.

**What this establishes.** Matures *Coeur d'Alene* into a usable rule rather than a sovereignty-interest exception; preserves prospective declaratory/injunctive relief while excluding retrospective compensation.

#### Verizon Maryland Inc. v. Public Service Commission, 535 U.S. 635 (2002)

Source: equality build, under "OT2001: decisions from late 2001 through 2002".

**Historical questions.** Did federal jurisdiction exist over the asserted federal-law challenge to the commission's order, and could prospective relief against commissioners proceed under *Ex parte Young*?

**Stone's disposition.** Vacate the dismissal and permit the properly framed federal claim to proceed.

**Reasoning.** The threshold officer-suit inquiry asks whether the complaint alleges an ongoing federal violation and seeks prospective relief. It does not require the claimant first to win the merits. The commission's own immunity and the lawfulness of its order remain distinct questions. [Opinion](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/535/635/).

#### Federal Maritime Commission v. South Carolina State Ports Authority, 535 U.S. 743 (2002)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2001".

**Status.** Historical Supreme Court merits case.

**Historical questions.** Does state sovereign immunity bar a privately filed administrative complaint before the FMC against a state port authority?

**Stone's disposition.** Vacate and remand; reject categorical immunity analysis.

**Reasoning.** A privately filed complaint does not automatically transform every component of an administrative proceeding into a forbidden private damages action. Separate the substantive federal shipping obligation, the legal/fiscal status of the Port Authority, the prospective compliance component, the retrospective reparations request, and the source authorizing each remedy. Prospective federal compliance adjudication can proceed where Congress clearly authorized it within an enumerated power; retrospective reparations require separate analysis of whether the Port is an arm of the State, a qualifying commercial state enterprise, or otherwise subject to a lawful compensatory route.

**What this establishes.** Close to a manifesto for Stone's sovereign-immunity method: never let the identity of the forum or plaintiff answer every remedial question at once.

#### Federal Maritime Commission v. South Carolina State Ports Authority, 535 U.S. 743 (2002)

Source: equality build, under "OT2001: decisions from late 2001 through 2002".

**Historical question.** Did state sovereign immunity bar the FMC from adjudicating a private party's Shipping Act complaint against the state port authority?

**Stone's disposition.** Reverse the appellate extension of immunity to this federal administrative proceeding and allow the statutory process to continue.

**Reasoning.** Stone follows the historical dissent's rejection of that extension into executive regulatory adjudication. The agency's authority and any resulting judicial enforcement must be identified under the statute. Do not assume a private complainant controls federal enforcement merely because it initiated the complaint. This position fits Stone's rejection of immunity as a general power to disregard valid federal obligations. [Opinion and dissents](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/535/743/).

#### United States v. Angle (merits)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2001".

**Status.** Lower-court sim case, major Commerce decision.

**Historical questions.** Does the interstate-commerce jurisdictional element for federal child-pornography possession survive when based only on the remote interstate travel of production materials, and can possession itself be included in a valid national trafficking prohibition?

**Stone's disposition.** Affirm the conviction, but reject the Government's remote-material theory.

**Reasoning.** First, a disk, camera, film, computer, videotape, or other production material does not give Congress perpetual jurisdiction over every later local act merely because the item once crossed State lines — the jurisdictional element must perform genuine constitutional work. Second, actual-child-pornography possession may nevertheless be included in a valid national interstate-trafficking regime where possession is sufficiently connected with acquisition, retention, copying, concealment, or redistribution that excluding it creates a material evasion route or materially impairs the trafficking prohibition.

**What this establishes.** The mature pre-*Raich* Commerce synthesis, combining *Lopez*'s limiting principle, *Hale*/*Rybar*'s anti-evasion rule, *Home Builders*/*Gibbs*'s mechanism-and-proof discipline, and *Fraternal Order*'s meaningful jurisdictional element. A genuinely self-contained local possession case cannot be federalized merely through the interstate history of incidental equipment.

### OT2002

Source: commerce build, text directly under "OT2002".

No mandatory core Commerce/Federalism roadmap case. This is intentional — a jurisprudence becomes unrealistic if every Term conveniently supplies a vehicle for doctrinal expansion. Existing law should be allowed to operate until a case actually presents a meaningful unresolved question.

### OT2003

#### Tennessee Student Assistance Corp. v. Hood, 541 U.S. 440 (2004)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2003".

**Status.** Stone roadmap.

**Historical questions.** Does the Bankruptcy Clause permit a bankruptcy court to discharge a student loan debt owed to a state agency without the State's consent?

**Stone's disposition.** Affirm bankruptcy discharge authority.

**Reasoning.** Stone should not say the Bankruptcy Clause generally destroys state sovereign immunity; instead characterize bankruptcy discharge according to what the proceeding actually does. Discharge works on the debtor, estate, and legal status of obligations within a centralized bankruptcy process — it is not automatically equivalent to an ordinary private retrospective damages judgment payable from a state treasury.

**What this establishes.** The narrow bankruptcy foundation needed for *Katz*, without wrecking the general Article I immunity architecture. Bankruptcy is exceptional because centralized disposition of the estate requires it, not because invoking Article I erases sovereign immunity generally.

### OT2004

#### Granholm v. Heald, 544 U.S. 460 (2005)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2004".

**Status.** Stone roadmap.

**Historical questions.** Do state laws permitting in-state wineries to ship directly to consumers while barring out-of-state wineries from doing the same survive dormant-Commerce scrutiny, notwithstanding the Twenty-first Amendment?

**Stone's disposition.** Invalidate the discriminatory direct-shipping systems.

**Reasoning.** The Twenty-first Amendment leaves States substantial authority over alcohol licensing, distribution, age verification, taxation, and public health, but does not authorize naked economic discrimination. If an in-state winery receives a commercially valuable distribution channel and a materially equivalent out-of-state winery is excluded because of geography, the State must justify the discrimination under the demanding dormant-Commerce rule.

**What this establishes.** Applies *Oregon Waste*/*Carbone*/*West Lynn* to a heavily regulated State field without erasing legitimate alcohol regulation.

#### Gonzales v. Raich, 545 U.S. 1 (2005)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2004".

**Status.** Stone roadmap, major Commerce case.

**Historical questions.** Does the Controlled Substances Act's application to purely intrastate, non-commercial cultivation and possession of marijuana exceed the commerce power?

**Stone's disposition.** Uphold the CSA application, narrowly.

**Reasoning.** Do not adopt an unlimited aggregation theory under which every homegrown product is "economic" because someone somewhere could buy it. The Act is a genuine national prohibition/regulation of an interstate fungible product market; home-grown marijuana is substitutable with market marijuana, and local cultivation and possession can provide concealment, diversion, substitution, and enforcement gaps that materially impair the national market prohibition. That fits *Hale → Rybar → Angle*, so Stone upholds it as an anti-evasion/material-impairment application of a valid interstate-market regime.

**What this establishes.** Proves Stone's Commerce jurisprudence is not anti-federal — a real national market receives strong federal power, and the limiting principle survives because the Government must identify the actual market regime and the mechanism through which exclusion of the local class would materially undermine it. Commerce chain at this point: *Lopez → Hale/Rybar → Home Builders/Gibbs → FOP → Angle → Raich.*

### OT2005

#### Central Virginia Community College v. Katz, 546 U.S. 356 (2006)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2005".

**Status.** Stone roadmap.

**Historical questions.** Does the Bankruptcy Clause permit a bankruptcy trustee to recover a preferential transfer from a state agency without the State's consent?

**Stone's disposition.** Permit the bankruptcy proceeding.

**Reasoning.** Build directly from *Hood*. Avoid the sweeping proposition that the States surrendered all sovereign immunity concerning bankruptcy at the Founding. The better rule is functional and source-bound: centralized bankruptcy sometimes requires recovery or administration of property belonging to the estate, and if fragmented state-controlled enforcement would prevent the bankruptcy court from accomplishing the constitutionally assigned bankruptcy function, with no adequate state-selected remedial channel able to perform that function, the federal bankruptcy process may operate.

**What this establishes.** Makes bankruptcy genuinely exceptional without making it doctrinally magical.

### OT2006

#### United Haulers Association v. Oneida-Herkimer Solid Waste Management Authority, 550 U.S. 330 (2007)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2006".

**Status.** Stone roadmap.

**Historical questions.** Does a flow-control ordinance directing waste to a publicly owned and operated facility survive dormant-Commerce scrutiny after *Carbone*?

**Stone's disposition.** Uphold genuine public-facility routing.

**Reasoning.** Do not overrule *Carbone*, which concerned coercive favoritism toward a private local enterprise. A genuinely publicly owned facility performing a traditional public waste-service function is different where local private and out-of-state private firms are treated alike — but public ownership is not conclusive; examine ownership, control, revenue, public-service obligations, fiscal responsibility, operation, and whether private local economic interests receive a hidden preference.

**What this establishes.** Completes *Carbone → SSC → Nashville/Waste Management → United Haulers.*

### OT2007

#### Department of Revenue of Kentucky v. Davis, 553 U.S. 328 (2008)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2007".

**Status.** Stone roadmap.

**Historical questions.** Does a state tax exemption favoring interest on its own municipal bonds over other States' bonds violate the dormant Commerce Clause?

**Stone's disposition.** Narrowly uphold the municipal-bond preference.

**Reasoning.** A State's treatment of debt issued to finance its own governmental operations is different from protection of resident private businesses against interstate competition. Stone should not announce a general "government exception" to the dormant Commerce Clause — the decision turns on the State financing its own governmental borrowing function.

**What this establishes.** Preserves actual State operational capacity while maintaining strong anti-protectionism toward private commerce.

### OT2008

Source: commerce build, text directly under "OT2008".

No mandatory core roadmap case. Again, no filler.

### OT2009

#### United States v. Comstock, 560 U.S. 126 (2010)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2009".

**Status.** Stone roadmap, Necessary and Proper.

**Historical questions.** Does Congress have authority under the Necessary and Proper Clause to civilly commit a mentally ill, sexually dangerous person already in federal custody beyond his release date?

**Stone's disposition.** Uphold § 4248 as applied to persons already lawfully in federal custody.

**Reasoning.** The reasoning must proceed through a finite chain: valid enumerated federal offense; lawful federal prosecution/custody; federal responsibility arising from that custody; limited measures reasonably tied to lawful and safe termination of that custody. This cannot become a general federal power to civilly confine dangerous persons — the statute must remain tied to the existing federal custodial relationship, qualifying condition/danger, meaningful process and review, and a genuine state-transfer mechanism.

**What this establishes.** Canonical Stone Necessary-and-Proper reasoning: enumerated end → incident means → identifiable limiting boundary.

### OT2010

#### Virginia Office for Protection & Advocacy v. Stewart, 563 U.S. 247 (2011)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2010".

**Status.** Stone roadmap.

**Historical questions.** Can a state agency created by the State itself sue another state officer for prospective relief under federal law?

**Stone's disposition.** Allow prospective officer relief.

**Reasoning.** The fact that the plaintiff itself was created by the State does not erase the ongoing federal duty of another State officer. Focus on the defendant, the present federal violation, the enforcement connection, and the prospective relief.

**What this establishes.** Applies *Coeur d'Alene → Verizon* without converting plaintiff identity into immunity doctrine.

#### Sossamon v. Texas, 563 U.S. 277 (2011)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2010".

**Status.** Stone roadmap.

**Historical questions.** Does a State's acceptance of federal funding under RLUIPA constitute consent to private damages actions against the State?

**Stone's disposition.** No private damages against the State on the asserted Spending Clause consent theory without unmistakable remedy notice.

**Reasoning.** Consent to federal funding conditions includes only what the State was clearly told it was accepting. A broad phrase such as "appropriate relief" should not automatically constitute unmistakable notice of private retrospective treasury damages.

**What this establishes.** Combines *Bradley* and *Kansas*: clear consent concerns not merely substantive obligations but the remedies to which the State agreed.

#### Bond v. United States (Bond I), 564 U.S. 211 (2011)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2010".

**Status.** Stone roadmap.

**Historical questions.** Does an individual criminal defendant have standing to raise a federalism-based challenge to the federal statute under which she is prosecuted?

**Stone's disposition.** Recognize individual standing to raise the federalism challenge; do not decide the Treaty/Commerce merits yet.

**Reasoning.** Federalism is not merely an institutional privilege belonging to State governments. Structural allocation protects individuals by limiting which government can exercise which power and by maintaining political accountability, so a person prosecuted under allegedly unauthorized federal law has a personal stake in the power question.

**What this establishes.** Standing only, preserving the substantive issue for a case that actually requires it.

### OT2011

#### Arizona v. United States, 567 U.S. 387 (2012)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2011".

**Status.** Stone roadmap, preemption.

**Historical questions.** Does federal immigration law preempt SB 1070's separate alien-registration offense, unauthorized-employment offense, warrantless arrest authority, and mandatory immigration-status inquiry?

**Stone's disposition.** Enjoin §§ 3, 5(C), and 6 as preempted. Reject a facial injunction against § 2(B), while preserving later as-applied challenges if implementation prolongs detention, creates an independent state removal regime, or otherwise conflicts with federal law.

**Reasoning.** Decide each enacted mechanism rather than rely on slogans about exclusive federal power or traditional state policing. Section 3 creates a parallel state registration crime in a field Congress comprehensively occupied. Section 5(C) adds a state criminal consequence for unauthorized employees where Congress deliberately chose a different allocation of sanctions. Section 6 gives state officers an independent arrest route tied to removability that interferes with the federal government's control over removal decisions and enforcement priorities. Section 2(B), by contrast, can on its face operate through federally authorized status inquiry and information sharing, so long as an otherwise lawful stop is not extended merely to pursue immigration verification and the State does not convert information exchange into its own removal system.

**What this establishes.** The culmination of *Easterwood → Myrick → Medtronic → Geier → Crosby → Arizona*: preemption turns on the actual federal rule, the state mechanism, and operational conflict. The same statute can therefore contain both displaced provisions and a provision that survives facial review subject to concrete implementation limits.

#### National Federation of Independent Business v. Sebelius, 567 U.S. 519 (2012)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2011".

**Status.** Stone roadmap, Commerce, Taxing, and Spending.

**Historical questions.** Does the Commerce Clause authorize the minimum-coverage requirement, does the associated payment fall within Congress's Taxing Power, and does the Medicaid-expansion funding condition unconstitutionally coerce the States?

**Stone's disposition, Commerce.** The minimum-coverage requirement is not supported by the Commerce Clause theory asserted.

**Reasoning, Commerce.** Stone should not create a metaphysical activity/inactivity slogan. The limiting problem is institutional and administrable: Congress may regulate commerce and conduct genuinely connected to a valid interstate regime, but it cannot create the commercial transaction that supplies its own asserted jurisdiction and then rely on that compelled transaction as the constitutional hook. A cost-shifting or inevitable-participation theory requires a demonstrated mechanism and boundary, not simply the proposition that everyone eventually uses healthcare.

**Stone's disposition, Taxing Power.** Uphold the minimum-coverage payment as an exercise of the Taxing Power.

**Reasoning, Taxing Power.** Decide what the provision legally and practically does. The payment is collected through the tax system, ordinarily depends on familiar tax variables, produces revenue for the Treasury, is capped well below the cost of purchasing insurance in many cases, carries no scienter requirement, and does not make remaining uninsured independently unlawful. Those features make it a tax that creates a financial incentive rather than a punitive command backed by ordinary regulatory sanctions. Functional tax analysis does not allow Congress to save any forbidden regulation by attaching the word "tax"; a charge that operates as a criminal or prohibitory penalty would present a different case.

**Stone's disposition, Medicaid.** Invalidate the threatened withdrawal of entrenched pre-existing Medicaid funds as the consequence of refusing the expansion, while permitting Congress to condition the genuinely new expansion funds on compliance with the expansion terms.

**Reasoning, Medicaid.** Apply *Kansas*'s practical-choice rule. Consider the size and entrenchment of the existing funding stream, the extent to which States built durable institutions around it, the magnitude and character of the new obligations, the availability of transition or replacement, and whether refusal remains a genuine governmental option rather than fiscal capitulation. Congress may offer a large new program and attach clear conditions to the new money; it may not use destruction of the established program as leverage that leaves no realistic choice.

**What this establishes.** Enumerated powers are analyzed separately and functionally. Failure under Commerce does not prevent a genuinely tax-like exaction from surviving under the Taxing Power, and broad Spending authority remains subject to an administrable practical-choice limit rather than a verbal distinction between "old" and "new" conditions alone.

### OT2012

Source: commerce build, text directly under "OT2012".

No mandatory Commerce/Federalism roadmap case. Do not invent one.

### OT2013

#### Bond v. United States (Bond II), 572 U.S. 844 (2014)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2013".

**Status.** Stone roadmap.

**Historical questions.** Does the federal Chemical Weapons Convention Implementation Act reach an ordinary local poisoning committed with a household chemical?

**Stone's disposition.** Reverse through narrow statutory interpretation if fairly available.

**Reasoning.** Use *Jones* and *SWANCC*. Do not casually construe a federal chemical-weapons statute so broadly that an ordinary local poisoning/assault automatically becomes an international chemical-weapons offense. If Congress unmistakably enacted that result, Stone may eventually have to reach the constitutional question, but if the statute fairly admits a narrower reading tied to the kind of chemical warfare and federally cognizable conduct it was enacted to reach, take the statutory route.

**What this establishes.** Stone's restraint at maturity: willing to decide a Treaty/Necessary-and-Proper question when necessary, not willing to manufacture necessity in order to decide it. Avoidance chain: *Jones → SWANCC → Bond II.*

### OT2014

Source: commerce build, text directly under "OT2014".

*Opinions in this Term's remaining roadmap cases occur in calendar year 2015, the end point of this planning horizon.*

#### Armstrong v. Exceptional Child Center, 575 U.S. 320 (2015)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2014".

**Status.** Stone roadmap.

**Historical questions.** Does the Supremacy Clause itself create a private cause of action to enforce a federal Medicaid rate-setting provision through prospective officer equity?

**Stone's disposition.** Reject the particular private Medicaid rate-setting action while preserving prospective officer equity generally.

**Reasoning.** The Supremacy Clause establishes priority of federal law; it does not itself create every desired cause of action. Ask separately whether the federal provision imposes a judicially administrable duty, whether Congress created or preserved a private remedial route, whether a specialized statutory enforcement system governs the same duty and requested relief, and whether ordinary prospective officer equity would duplicate or undermine that specific arrangement. The particular Medicaid rate-setting structure can foreclose this action without announcing that prospective equitable enforcement of federal law generally disappears.

**What this establishes.** Preserves *Coeur d'Alene*/*VOPA*/*Verizon* while taking statutory remedial design seriously.

#### Comptroller of the Treasury of Maryland v. Wynne, 575 U.S. 542 (2015)

Source: commerce build, under "OT2014".

**Status.** Stone roadmap.

**Historical questions.** Does Maryland's personal income tax scheme, which does not fully credit tax paid to other States, discriminate against interstate commerce?

**Stone's disposition.** Invalidate the discriminatory interstate tax structure.

**Reasoning.** Use internal consistency as a diagnostic rather than a mathematical ritual: if every State adopted the same tax structure, would identical interstate activity systematically bear an additional tax burden that identical intrastate activity avoids? If yes, the structure penalizes interstate commerce because it is interstate.

**What this establishes.** The culmination of the taxation chain *Kraft/Itel → Jefferson Lines → Wynne.* States retain broad tax authority; what they cannot do is design the tax system so that crossing a State border itself produces a systematic additional burden.

## Second Amendment

### Area-wide standards and notes

#### Justice Stone: Second Amendment build, OT1994 through OT2015

Source: Second Amendment build, its title and opening text.

**Simulation handoff. Proposed positions, not predetermined Court holdings.**

#### Scope and how to use this build

Source: Second Amendment build, top-level section.

This build runs from *United States v. Lopez* in OT1994 through OT2015 and now deliberately develops a fuller mature doctrine rather than stopping at the home-defense core. It remains a stable individual-right doctrine, not a maximal all-weapons presumption: ordinary arms and lawful self-defense receive strong protection, while demonstrated dangerous persons, dangerous and unusual weapons, specially regulated weapon categories, objective registration, classification-changing modifications, sensitive places, and genuine safety administration remain regulable. The planning instruction still rejects adding a case merely because an October Term otherwise lacks a gun decision; the added vehicles below perform specific doctrinal work that the project now affirmatively wants.

**Existing record note.** The formal simulation record presently reaches the early-2000s portion of this project; the later vehicles in this file are roadmap positions, not silently imported holdings. *Heller I*, *Heller II*, *Ezell*, *Moore*, *Jackson*, *Bonidy*, and *Heller III* therefore state the disposition and reasoning Stone should take if and when those vehicles are selected. There is one inherited record issue worth preserving rather than silently repairing: the controlling handoff instruction treats the Second Amendment as an individual and incorporated right by the end of OT1999, while the earlier *Love* and *Hale* writings did not themselves produce a clean controlling incorporation holding. The roadmap treats incorporation as an operational simulation premise rather than taking *McDonald* solely to fix recordkeeping.

**Status tags.** "Essential" means the case is load-bearing for the architecture now requested. "Recommended" means it materially improves the doctrine and should ordinarily be taken if the vehicle remains clean. "Optional" remains a genuine refinement rather than filler. "Deliberate silence" means Stone should not manufacture a gun case merely to occupy an October Term.

#### The standards Stone is building

Source: Second Amendment build, top-level section.

**Status note.** These standards describe Stone's mature target architecture. A proposition is not controlling simulation law merely because it appears here; before the case that supplies the relevant vehicle, it is a planned position. The case sections below identify what has actually been established, what remains proposed, and what should remain open.

##### S1. The sequence for every Second Amendment case

Stone does not adopt a universal scrutiny tier. For every case he asks, in order: Who is claiming the right? What acquisition, possession, carriage, use, transfer, component, or modification is regulated? What arm or device is involved? What lawful purpose is asserted? Where does the conduct occur? Does an established dangerous-person, weapon-category, sensitive-place, commercial, or safety branch apply? What process supports any disqualification? What does the rule do in practical operation? Does it administer the right or destroy the protected application? If federal law is involved, what enumerated power independently supports it? What remedy is necessary? What questions can honestly be left undecided?

History supplies regulatory principles and boundaries, not a demand for an exact historical twin and not a license to reason at unlimited generality. A court must identify the concrete principle that made the earlier regulation permissible and compare the modern rule's reason and burden.

##### S2. The ordinary right-holder

The right belongs to an individual natural person. The presumptive claimant is an adult not subject to a constitutionally sufficient disqualification who seeks an ordinary arm for a lawful purpose. "Law-abiding," "responsible," and "dangerous" are descriptive terms, not blank checks. Government must identify the legal predicate that removes a claimant from the ordinary category.

Arrest, accusation, unpopular association, administrative suspicion, an untested report, diagnosis alone, or a generalized risk score does not itself extinguish the right.

##### S3. Protected purposes and the home-defense core

Immediate lawful self-defense is central, especially in the home, but the constitutional field also includes peaceable possession, lawful acquisition, ordinary maintenance, transportation, training, hunting, sporting use, and other traditionally lawful uses.

Government may not prohibit every ordinary handgun, force every lawful home firearm to remain continuously unusable, make lawful acquisition practically impossible, use licensing as a discretionary veto, or eliminate ordinary protected arms one category at a time while pointing to substitutes.

The Amendment protects possession, readiness, and carriage; it does not create a substantive license to assault, threaten, fire recklessly, trespass while armed after lawful exclusion, resist lawful arrest, or use force when ordinary self-defense law forbids it.

##### S4. Ordinary arms

Ordinary handguns are protected. Ordinary semiautomatic rifles and shotguns do not become constitutionally equivalent to machineguns because of appearance, military ancestry, detachable magazines, pistol grips, adjustable stocks, or a legislative label.

Classification turns on operation and function: whether the arm fires one round per trigger activation or continuously; whether its destructive effect materially differs from ordinary individual firearms; whether it is commonly possessed or ordinarily suited to lawful civilian use; whether the challenged features materially alter operation rather than appearance; and whether government itself suppressed possession and then relies on that suppression to prove uncommonness.

Government may not prohibit an ordinary protected arm merely because another protected arm remains available.

##### S5. Dangerous and unusual weapons; specially regulated categories

The right does not protect every object that can linguistically be called an arm. Under *Hale* and *Rybar*, post-1986 civilian fully automatic machineguns may be prohibited. Weapons whose ordinary function is explosive or area-destructive rather than ordinary individual defense may likewise fall outside the core or be subjected to exceptional regulation.

Between ordinary arms and prohibitable weapons is a specially regulated tier. Government may require enhanced registration, transfer controls, identifying information, secure transport, and other objective conditions for categories defined by material functional characteristics. Suppressors or silencers, short-barreled rifle or shotgun configurations, destructive-device categories, and comparable historically distinct classes may be regulated in this manner without automatically treating every item in the tier as constitutionally unprotected.

"Dangerous" alone is insufficient because every firearm is dangerous. "Unusual" alone is insufficient because government cannot manufacture constitutional authority by first suppressing an arm and then citing the suppression as proof of unusualness.

##### S6. Modifications, accessories, and regulated components

Ordinary maintenance, repair, replacement of ordinary components, sights, lights, grips, slings, cosmetic changes, and comparable accessories do not become separate constitutional events merely because they alter a firearm.

A different rule applies when a modification materially changes the legal or functional category of the weapon. Government may require advance approval or registration when a modification creates a specially regulated short-barreled configuration, adds a separately regulated suppressor, converts an ordinary firearm into a prohibited automatic category, incorporates a separately regulated destructive device, or otherwise changes the weapon into a category that could lawfully have been specially regulated if manufactured that way originally.

The registration may attach to the regulated component, the resulting weapon configuration, or both when that is reasonably necessary to identify the regulated item. The owner may be required to update the host firearm's record after a material classification change. Government may not require separate registration of every ordinary replacement part or accessory merely because it can attach to a firearm.

Any modification regime must be objective, reasonably prompt, accessible, and reviewable. Administrators may not possess open-ended authority to deny a modification that satisfies the governing legal conditions.

##### S7. Person-based disqualification: governing principle

The Second Amendment permits defined disqualification of persons whose adjudicated conduct or constitutionally sufficient proceedings establish a materially heightened danger of firearm misuse. The strength and duration of the disability must track the predicate that justifies it.

Government may regulate demonstrated dangerousness; it may not convert every criminal label, diagnosis, status, or accusation into permanent civil death for the right.

##### S8. Violent convictions and restoration

A constitutionally adequate criminal conviction may support durable disarmament when the offense itself establishes intentional violence, attempted violence, a credible threat of serious physical injury, armed criminal conduct, stalking accompanied by qualifying threats, misuse of a deadly weapon, or comparable demonstrated danger.

The criminal adjudication supplies process and proof beyond a reasonable doubt, so government need not relitigate present dangerousness at periodic intervals merely to enforce the disability while the conviction and its legally prescribed consequences remain operative.

"Durable" does not necessarily mean constitutionally irreversible for life. A pardon, expungement, set-aside, successful collateral attack, restoration statute, or other legal mechanism that removes the predicate's force matters. A future as-applied challenge involving a remote conviction, completed sentence, long period of lawful conduct, and no other danger evidence remains open. A legislature may not make the word "felony" alone do all the constitutional work for a nonviolent regulatory offense.

##### S9. Civil protective orders and emergency risk orders

A civil protective order may support temporary disarmament when the restrained person received actual notice, had a meaningful opportunity to participate, and a neutral judicial officer entered a current danger-linked order after constitutionally adequate process. The disability ends when the qualifying order ends unless another sufficient predicate exists.

Emergency ex parte authority may exist for a genuine immediate threat, but it must remain temporary. A sworn particularized showing to a neutral judicial officer, prompt adversarial review, a heightened burden for any continuing order, fixed duration, meaningful renewal standards, and prompt return or lawful transfer of firearms when the predicate ends are central safeguards.

A generic divorce injunction, routine mutual civility order, accusation alone, expired order, or administrative notation does not automatically satisfy this branch.

##### S10. Mental-health and substance-use predicates

A psychiatric diagnosis, voluntary treatment, medication, disability status, or counseling does not by itself extinguish the right. Government may impose a temporary or durable disability following a constitutionally adequate adjudication or qualifying commitment that establishes serious danger to self or others. The duration of the restriction and access to restoration must track the dangerousness predicate.

Government may prohibit carrying or using firearms while presently intoxicated or impaired to a degree that materially compromises safe handling. Addiction or substance use may support a disability when adjudicated conduct or reliable individualized evidence establishes concrete danger. Mere status or regular use, standing alone, should not automatically create permanent disarmament.

##### S11. Licensing and registration

Objective, timely, reviewable, and nonprohibitory licensing and registration may coexist with the right. Reasonable administration may include identity verification, background checks, one-time fingerprints or photographs when genuinely useful, accessible training, registration of ordinary firearms, and more detailed registration of specially regulated arms or classification-changing modifications.

For ordinary firearms, registration should ordinarily be simple, inexpensive, ministerial, and stable. Government may require updates for change of address, transfer, loss, theft, or a material classification-changing modification rather than forcing repeated re-registration of the same unchanged person and firearm without demonstrated need.

Licensing fails when its actual operation produces arbitrary denial, open-ended discretion, extraordinary-need requirements, indefinite delay, cumulative unaffordable cost, impossible conditions, repeated unsupported demands, or practical prohibition.

##### S12. Serial numbers and traceability

Government may require commercially manufactured firearms and separately regulated weapons or components to bear durable identifying information reasonably connected to lawful tracing, transfer administration, and enforcement of valid ownership restrictions. There is no constitutional right to deliberately obliterate or falsify a required identifying mark.

Personal manufacture for lawful personal use is not automatically outside the Second Amendment. Government may nevertheless require objective serialization or registration of a completed personally manufactured firearm when it enters commerce or when it falls within a specially regulated category, provided lawful compliance is practically available.

A technical paperwork error is not automatically equivalent to violent misuse. Knowledge, notice, opportunity to cure, and the seriousness of the violation matter when criminal penalties attach to administrative requirements.

##### S13. Acquisition, dealers, transfers, and waiting periods

A meaningful right to keep arms includes practical lawful acquisition. Government may license dealers, require commercial recordkeeping and background checks, prohibit sales to legally disqualified persons, punish straw purchasing and trafficking, and require accessible screening for private transfers.

A short, objective waiting period may be permissible when genuinely connected to screening or a defined safety function. Indefinite delay, repetitive waiting periods without a current function, or administration designed to defeat otherwise lawful acquisition is not.

Commercial regulation becomes unconstitutional when government nominally permits possession while eliminating every practical lawful channel through which an ordinary qualified person can acquire a protected arm or ordinary ammunition.

##### S14. Safe storage and unauthorized access

Government may require reasonable precautions against unauthorized access by children, prohibited persons, or others when a firearm is unattended.

The rule must preserve meaningful defensive access by the lawful possessor. Government may not require the only firearm available for home defense to remain continuously disabled while the lawful possessor is present and reasonably relying on it for immediate defense.

Negligent or reckless storage can be regulated without converting every unauthorized access into strict criminal liability regardless of reasonable precautions.

##### S15. Training and ranges

Government may require basic, genuinely accessible firearms-safety training as an objective condition of public carriage or another regulated activity. It may regulate instructor qualifications, range location, construction, ventilation, lead containment, noise, hours, storage, security, and ordinary business operation.

Government may not require live-fire training while simultaneously prohibiting every practical means of obtaining it. The Amendment does not require government-financed instruction, a preferred range, advanced tactical training, or recreational shooting on demand.

##### S16. Ammunition and feeding devices

The right to possess an operable firearm necessarily includes practical access to ordinary ammunition suitable for that firearm. Government may regulate particularly hazardous ammunition, commercial sale, storage, and unlawful use, but it may not eliminate every practical source of ordinary ammunition for protected arms.

Magazines integral to the ordinary operation of protected semiautomatic firearms fall within the constitutional field. The Constitution contains no numerical magazine-capacity limit. Classification must use the record: ordinary factory configurations, prevalence in lawful possession, ordinary lawful use, effect on operation, the characteristics of unusually large drums or other materially distinct feeding devices, and the safety mechanism asserted by government.

Ordinary magazines cannot be banned merely because materially different unusually large devices may be specially regulated.

##### S17. Public carriage

"Bear arms" protects peaceable carriage of an ordinary firearm for lawful self-defense outside the home. A State may regulate manner and may require an objective permit, but ordinary qualified adults must retain a practical lawful means of public carriage.

A State need not constitutionalize every manner of carry simultaneously. It may choose reasonable open- or concealed-carry rules, training requirements, identification rules, and manner restrictions so long as the system does not amount to a general prohibition or discretionary veto.

Extraordinary-need, political approval, personal-connection, or open-ended "good character" requirements are incompatible with an ordinary right when they let officials deny qualified applicants for reasons not tied to an established disqualification.

##### S18. Sensitive places and private property

Government may prohibit firearms in a genuinely sensitive place when the location's function, custody, vulnerability, or security arrangements materially distinguish it from ordinary public space. Courthouses, secure portions of government buildings, jails and prisons, polling places while voting is occurring, and schools involving minors are strong candidates.
